- **fsa-ch01-introduction**: Frames software architecture as four interlocking dimensions (characteristics, logical components, style, decisions) analyzed in that order, applies three laws (trade-offs everywhere, rationale over mechanism, decisions on a spectrum) and the eight expectations of an architect. Based on Chapter 1 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when scoping an architecture effort, separating architectural from technical decisions, challenging "no trade-off" claims, checking decision compliance or vitality, or coaching someone growing into the architect role. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch01-introduction))
- **fsa-ch02-architectural-thinking**: Applies the architectural-thinking mindset from Chapter 2 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers placing a decision on the architecture-versus-design spectrum, two-sided trade-off analysis, translating business drivers into architecture characteristics, building technical breadth (knowledge pyramid, daily learning habit, personal technology radar), and staying hands-on without becoming a bottleneck. Use when deciding who owns a decision, comparing options, planning an architect's learning, or advising a developer moving into architecture. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch02-architectural-thinking))
- **fsa-ch03-modularity**: Modularity analysis for architects, based on Chapter 3 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers modularity versus granularity, the cohesion scale, LCOM, afferent/efferent coupling, abstractness, instability, distance from the main sequence (zones of pain and uselessness) and connascence (forms, strength, locality, degree). Use when sizing or splitting modules or services, reviewing coupling, assessing a code base before a migration, measuring technical debt, or naming a coupling problem in a code or design review. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch03-modularity))
- **fsa-ch04-characteristics-defined**: Defines what counts as an architecture characteristic, how to tell it apart from domain requirements and ordinary design hygiene, how to categorize and name characteristics (operational, structural, cloud, cross-cutting, ISO families), and why to keep the set small and accept least-worst trade-offs. Based on Chapter 4 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when sorting a requirements list into domain vs. capabilities, deciding whether a concern needs structural support, building a shared glossary of "-ilities", or pruning an overlong list of quality goal… ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch04-characteristics-defined))
- **fsa-ch05-identifying-characteristics**: Guides identification of architecture characteristics ("-ilities") from domain concerns, requirements and implicit domain knowledge; decomposing composite goals like agility; telling scalability from elasticity; deciding architecture versus design; and pruning to a short prioritized list via a worksheet and top-three selection. Based on Chapter 5 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when gathering non-functional requirements, translating business goals into -ilities, running an architecture kata, or running a stakeholder prioritization session. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch05-identifying-characteristics))
- **fsa-ch06-measuring-and-governing**: Turns vague architecture characteristics into objective operational, structural and process measures, and governs them with automated fitness functions (cycle checks, layer rules, main-sequence distance, complexity gates, chaos-style production checks). Based on Chapter 6 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when defining measurable "-ilities", choosing latency or deployability metrics, setting Cyclomatic Complexity thresholds, writing architecture tests for CI, or stopping modularity erosion. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch06-measuring-and-governing))
- **fsa-ch07-scope-of-characteristics**: Scopes architecture characteristics with the architecture quantum (independently deployable, cohesive unit with its data) and uses that scope to pick monolithic vs distributed styles, draw service boundaries, choose persistence and sync vs async communication, and scope cloud-hosted parts. Based on Chapter 7 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed). Use when deciding whether one set of characteristics fits the whole system, finding quanta, spotting shared-database or shared-library coupling, or diagnosing failing calls between services with mismatched scalabilit… ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch07-scope-of-characteristics))
- **fsa-ch08-component-based-thinking**: Guides identifying, sizing and refining logical components and analyzing coupling between them, based on Chapter 8 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when drawing a logical architecture before a physical one, seeding components with the Workflow or Actor/Action approach, spotting Entity Trap "Manager" components, assigning user stories, splitting components for cohesion or architecture characteristics, computing afferent/efferent coupling, finding temporal coupling, or applying the Law of Demeter. - **fsa-ch10-layered-style**: Guidance for evaluating, designing and governing the layered (n-tier) architecture style, based on Chapter 10 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers logical and physical layer topology, open vs closed layers, layers of isolation, the Architecture Sinkhole 80/20 check, cloud, risk, governance and team fit. Covers single-repo vs per-module artifacts, peer-to-peer vs mediator communication, data topology, risks, fitness functions and team alignment. Use when choosing a starting architecture, restructuring a layered monolith by domain, reviewing module boundaries, writing boundary checks, or deciding when a monolith has outgrown itself. - **fsa-ch12-pipeline-style**: Guidance for evaluating, designing and governing the pipeline (pipes and filters) architecture style, based on Chapter 12 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). - **fsa-ch16-space-based-style**: Guidance for evaluating, designing and governing space-based architectures (processing units, replicated in-memory data grids, data pumps, readers and writers, elastic deployment), based on Chapter 16 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture". Use when a system faces extreme or spiky concurrency, when choosing replicated versus distributed caching, estimating data-collision risk, planning cache-to-database sync, defining fitness functions for memory and sync time, aligning teams, or deciding whether the style is worth its cost and complexity. - **fsa-ch17-orchestration-driven-soa**: Guidance on orchestration-driven service-oriented architecture (SOA) and its modern legacy, based on Chapter 17 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture". Covers the layered service taxonomy, orchestration engines and ESBs, reuse-versus-coupling trade-offs, canonical entity pitfalls, single-quantum characteristics, the Accidental SOA antipattern, and fitness functions for integration hubs. - **fsa-ch18-microservices**: Applies the microservices architecture style as described in Chapter 18 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). - **fsa-ch19-choosing-a-style**: Guides selection of an architecture style (monolith vs distributed, layered, modular monolith, microkernel, microservices, event-driven, space-based, service-based) from domain knowledge, architecture characteristics, data, cloud, organizational and team factors, and domain/architecture shape fit. Based on Chapter 19 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). - **fsa-ch20-architectural-patterns**: Applies Chapter 20 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on architectural patterns as contextual solutions distinct from styles, best practices and tools. Covers the Covering Your Assets, Groundhog Day and Email-Driven Architecture antipatterns, the last responsible moment, architectural significance, and Architectural Decision Records (sections, status and RFC, approval criteria, compliance, storage, standards, legacy systems, AI use). Use when deciding whether or when to decide, writing or reviewing an ADR, setting approval rules, or asking an LLM for architecture advice. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch21-architectural-decisions))
- **fsa-ch22-analyzing-risk**: Applies Chapter 22 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on analyzing architecture risk. Covers the impact x likelihood risk matrix, risk assessments by characteristic and domain with totals, filtering and trend direction, three-phase risk storming (identification, consensus, mitigation), unknown-technology scoring, cost-versus-risk negotiation, user-story risk and mitigation patterns. Use when scoring a risk, building a risk report, facilitating or aggregating a risk-storming session, or proposing mitigations. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch22-analyzing-risk))
- **fsa-ch23-diagramming**: Applies Chapter 23 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on diagramming architecture. Covers representational consistency across views, the Irrational Artifact Attachment antipattern, low- versus high-fidelity artifacts, tool features and semantic layers, UML, C4 and ArchiMate, and guidelines for titles, lines, shapes, labels, color and keys. Dispara em "quebrou", "erro", "não funciona", stack trace, ou algo lento/falhando. Não use para feature nova (fsk-plan/fsk-build) nem para só validar o que já funciona (fsk-prove). ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-debug))
- **fsk-docs**: Gera documentação, apresentações e o tour guiado da mudança no padrão do projeto — relatório de evidências, dossiê de revisão, feature, API, runbook, notas de release, apresentação, relatório executivo. Markdown é escrito direto; formatos binários via docgen MCP quando o projeto o tem, senão pelas skills oficiais de documento (docgen é opcional). Dispara em "documenta", "gera doc/apresentação/relatório", "me guia pela mudança", "tour". Não use para provar (fsk-prove) nem para abrir PR (fsk-ship). Idioma dos artefatos segue o do repo. ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-docs))
- **fsk-explore**: Levanta contexto de uma área e devolve ponteiros (arquivo:linha), nunca conteúdo bruto. Dispara quando o usuário pergunta "onde fica X", "como funciona Y", "o que isso afeta", ou numa área desconhecida ou de impacto amplo antes de planejar/implementar — mas só com pergunta que o Mapa/AGENTS.md do setup NÃO responde (o que o Mapa aponta não se explora de novo). Não use para planejar, implementar, revisar ou provar — só para descobrir e apontar. ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-explore))
- **fsk-learn**: Transforma aprendizado durável em regra viva — convenção do time vira rule/how-to no projeto (por PR, com evidência e carimbo), preferência pessoal vira nota global do usuário, e o Mapa nunca fica falso. Dispara PROATIVAMENTE, no mesmo turno, sempre que algo durável aparece — resposta de grill/entrevista que vale além da demanda, procedimento executado que vai se repetir (vira how-to), correção do usuário, "sempre faça X aqui", padrão repetido, decisão com porquê, drift entre rule e código, e no fecho de TODA fase (plan/build/prove/review/ship/debug/setup) — varre sempre, persiste só o duráve… ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-learn))
- **fsk-plan**: Classifica a demanda por tipo × tamanho × risco, entrevista até o entendimento compartilhado (Combinado) e entrega o plano de ataque que o build executa. Dispara em demanda nova acima de trivial ("quero", "precisamos", "adicionar", "mudar", "criar"). Não use para implementar (é /fsk-build), para bug já localizado (fsk-debug) nem para dúvida de código (fsk-explore). ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-plan))
- **fsk-prove**: Prova comportamento de verdade e sem viés — navegador &gt; API real &gt; integração &gt; unitário (só com motivo escrito); checagem (tsc/lint/unitário) nunca é prova. Lê os critérios antes do diff; em trabalho de risco alto roda em subagente que não viu a implementação. Dispara em "valida", "testa", "funciona?", ao terminar/fechar uma mudança de código e no fechamento de cada fatia. Não use para revisar código (fsk-review) nem para migrar a suíte existente. ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-prove))
- **fsk-review**: Verifica a mudança contra o plano e as diretrizes — fidelidade (nada faltou, nada fora), regras, segurança, impacto, simplicidade; bloqueia regra de negócio alterada sem aprovação e reverte/ajusta ruído, overengineering e antipadrão no diff. Dispara em "revisa", "está bom?", "review". Não use para provar comportamento (fsk-prove) nem para abrir PR (fsk-ship). ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-review))
- **fsk-setup**: Prepara o repositório para o workflow fsk — detecta o modo (novo/brownfield/auditoria), explora com evidência, entrevista o que o repo não responde (inclusive o fluxo de git do time), gera AGENTS.md + rules + estrutura .claude e propõe ferramentas/scripts com "ok". Dispara quando o repo não tem AGENTS.md ou o usuário pede para configurar/preparar o projeto. Não use para planejar nem implementar demanda — isso é /fsk-plan e /fsk-build. ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-setup))
- **fsk-ship**: Entrega a mudança seguindo o fluxo de git do time à risca — dossiê de revisão, rodada final "está 100%?", push/PR no formato do repo com evidência e Mapa atualizado no mesmo PR. Dispara por "abre PR", "entrega", "manda pro main", "push" (o agente sugere, nunca aciona sozinho; o commit local é do /fsk-build). Não use para planejar/implementar (fsk-plan/fsk-build) nem só para revisar (fsk-review). ([KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit](https://github.com/KauaLealz/fullstack-skills-kit/tree/HEAD/skills/fsk-ship))
